========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. Not constantly, exactly, but more often than I’d like. There are times when I’m just sitting in my own living room — nothing urgent happening, no one waiting on me, no particular decision to make — and I still feel sort of wound up inside.

It’s almost embarrassing because I’ll think, “What am I even reacting to right now?” I might be on the phone and notice I’m tapping my fingers the whole time, or I’ll get up and straighten a stack of papers, or move a cushion, or fuss with something on the table, not because it truly needs doing but because sitting quietly feels oddly uncomfortable. Like my body wants me to be doing something, even if there isn’t anything that actually needs doing.

So yes, it can be there even without a clear reason. It’s this jumpy, restless feeling, as if I’m waiting for something to need my attention.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what's been going on that brought you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally got me to come in is that I can’t seem to turn the worrying off once it gets started. I’ll tell myself, “All right, I’ve made the list, I checked the dates, I’ve done what I can reasonably do,” and part of me knows that should be enough. But then my mind just keeps going back over it anyway, like maybe I missed something, or maybe I should check again, or think it through one more time.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t try to be practical about things—I do. I write things down, I try to be organized, I remind myself there’s nothing more to do at the moment. But the worry sort of keeps running in the background, and sometimes in the foreground, even after I’ve done all the sensible steps. That’s really why I’m here. I’m tired of feeling like I can’t just put it down.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what kinds of things you worry about when your mind keeps going back to them?\n\nPatient: It can be a lot of different things, and that’s part of what makes it feel so silly and exhausting at the same time. My mind doesn’t just stay on one topic. It sort of roams.\n\nFor example, I’ll start thinking about the exhibition schedule — I help keep track of dates and who needs to be where, and whether something has been confirmed — and then before I’ve even finished with that thought, I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans. Like, did she leave enough time between connections, is the weather going to interfere, should I remind her about something even though she’s perfectly capable and doesn’t need me hovering.\n\nAnd then somehow it jumps to the house, like whether the gutters need fixing, or whether I should have called someone already, or if waiting will turn into water damage or some bigger problem. It’s not that any one thing is necessarily terrible on its own. It’s more that small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger if I don’t keep track of them carefully enough. And then I’m off to the races, mentally.\n\nDoctor: When your mind starts worrying like this, do you feel driven to do certain things—like checking, counting, or repeating actions—to try to reduce the anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, exactly. I mean, I do sometimes go back and look at a calendar again, or reread an email, or make another note for myself, but it doesn’t feel like… counting or doing something in a very specific ritual way, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll feel uneasy and think, “Maybe I should just make sure,” and then I check because I’m worried I overlooked something. But I don’t have a particular number of times I have to do it, or a set pattern, or anything like that. It’s more ordinary checking that maybe goes on longer than it needs to because my mind won’t quite let it rest.\n\nI suppose sometimes the checking gives me a little relief, but not for very long, because then I can think of some other angle I didn’t consider. So… no, I don’t think I’m driven to repeat actions in that kind of way. It’s mostly the thinking and rethinking that gets me stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel constant or almost daily, and have they been happening for most days over the past several months?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I’d say it’s been most days. Not every single minute of every day, but close enough that it feels like it’s become part of the background of my life.\n\nIt really started about seven months ago, I think—shortly after my friend died. We had been involved in some projects together, and after she was gone I felt this need to keep things moving, to make sure nothing fell apart or got forgotten. At first I thought it was just grief, or responsibility, or both, and maybe some of it was. But then the worrying just sort of stayed. It kept attaching itself to one thing after another.\n\nSo yes, for the past several months—about seven months—it’s been happening on most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any thoughts that come into your mind that you don’t want or that feel intrusive, that cause you anxiety or distress, even if you try to push them away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I usually understand “intrusive thoughts,” at least. I mean, I do have thoughts I don’t want, in the sense that I wish my brain would stop bringing up all these worries again and again. But they don’t feel like strange thoughts that pop in out of nowhere, or images that shock me, or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like I start worrying about something ordinary, and then it gets sticky. I’ll try to move on and tell myself, “You’ve already thought about this,” but then my mind circles back anyway. So the thoughts are unwanted, yes, because they’re tiring and upsetting, but they feel connected to real-life concerns rather than like some separate, intrusive thing.\n\nI’m not sure if that distinction makes sense. I may be splitting hairs. But no, I haven’t really noticed thoughts that feel alien or bizarre or like I’m fighting off something completely out of character. It’s more the same worries coming back and refusing to quiet down.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself mentally going over things in a specific way—like mentally reviewing events in detail, silently repeating certain words or phrases, or trying to 'neutralize' worrisome thoughts with other thoughts—in order to feel less anxious or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think about that carefully, because I don’t want to say no too quickly if I’m missing what you mean.\n\nI do go over things in my mind, definitely. I’ll replay a conversation or a decision or something I’m supposed to remember, and I can get caught in that for longer than is probably useful. But it doesn’t feel like I’m doing it in a specific, set way, if that makes sense. I’m not silently repeating a phrase to cancel something out, or thinking a “good” thought to undo a “bad” one, or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I’m trying to reassure myself by thinking it through one more time. And then one more time. But there isn’t a rule to it. I don’t have to review it in a certain order, or until it feels exactly right, or to prevent something terrible in a superstitious way. It’s just that my mind keeps wanting another pass at it.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I mentally review things, yes, but not in the kind of ritualized or neutralizing way you’re describing. It feels more like worry that won’t settle down.\n\nDoctor: Has this constant worrying affected your ability to function in your daily life, like at work, with your family, or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, though I’d say it’s not… it hasn’t stopped my life, exactly. I’m still doing things. I still keep up with the garden, and I still go to book club, so from the outside I probably look mostly the same.\n\nBut I can tell I’m not functioning quite like I used to. I’ll arrive at book club less prepared than I normally would, like I’ve read the chapter but haven’t really gathered my thoughts, or I’ve forgotten the notes I meant to bring. And with the exhibition planning, I’ve started stepping back from pieces of it. I’ll turn down certain tasks or say I can’t take on a part of it, not because I don’t care, but because it just feels harder to manage than it used to.\n\nIt’s subtle, maybe, but it bothers me because I used to be someone who could keep those things organized. Now I still show up, but I feel less capable and less ready.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like restlessness, feeling on edge, fatigue, or muscle tension that seem to go along with this worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have noticed that, mostly as this sort of jumpy, wound-up feeling. It’s not dramatic, exactly, but it’s there. I’ll be sitting in my own living room, where there’s no reason to feel keyed up, and I’ll realize I’m tapping my fingers on the arm of the chair or on the phone while I’m talking to someone.\n\nAnd sometimes I just can’t seem to sit still comfortably. I’ll get up and straighten a cushion or move a stack of papers or adjust something on a shelf, not because it really needs doing, but because sitting quietly makes me feel… uneasy, I suppose. Like my body is looking for something to do while my mind is still worrying.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m pacing around the house all day, but there is this sense of being on edge in small ways. It’s almost embarrassing, because it’s my own home and nothing is happening, but I still feel wound up.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, would you say that your level of worry and anxiety has gone beyond what would be expected in response to the situations you're concerned about, or has it felt more like a natural reaction to real-life stresses?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, looking back, it has gone beyond what the situations really call for. I mean, the things I worry about are real things, so I can always make an argument to myself that I’m just being responsible. There is an exhibition schedule, and my daughter does have travel plans, and the gutters may actually need looking at. Those aren’t imaginary.\n\nBut the way my mind treats them feels out of proportion. It doesn’t just stay with one concern and solve it. It sort of roams from one thing to the next—the exhibition schedule, then whether my daughter’s flight will go smoothly, then whether the house gutters are going to turn into some expensive problem—and before I know it, very ordinary things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger.\n\nThat’s the part that makes me think it isn’t just a natural reaction. It’s like small uncertainties don’t stay small in my head. They gather weight. And even when I know, logically, that none of these things is a disaster at the moment, my worry behaves as if they might become one.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself especially worried about your health or physical symptoms, to the point where it causes you a lot of anxiety even if medical evaluations have told you there's nothing seriously wrong?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not in that specific way. I mean, at my age I suppose you do notice your body more than you used to, and if something aches or feels odd, I might think, “Oh, what’s that about?” But I wouldn’t say I get especially consumed by health worries or keep thinking something serious is being missed.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll make a mental note of something and then it usually passes into the background. I don’t think I’ve had that pattern of being reassured and then still being terribly anxious about it. At least, not that I can think of. My worries tend to attach themselves more to responsibilities and things that feel like they could go wrong, rather than to my health specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it significantly difficult to control the worry, even when you know it might not be reasonable or helpful?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s really the thing that brings me in, I suppose. It isn’t just that I worry — everyone worries — it’s that once it starts, I can’t seem to turn it off.\n\nI’ll tell myself, quite firmly sometimes, “All right, you’ve made the list, you’ve checked the dates, you’ve done what can reasonably be done.” And for a few minutes I might believe that. But then the worry sort of starts up again anyway, almost like a radio left on in another room. I know it isn’t necessarily helping anything, and I can even see that I’m going over the same ground, but I still can’t quite let it go.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I don’t feel as though I’m choosing to keep thinking about it. I’ll try to redirect myself or say, “Enough now,” but my mind keeps circling back, looking for one more thing I might have missed.\n\nDoctor: Would you say the anxiety and worry you're experiencing has caused you significant distress or has it interfered with your ability to live your life the way you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has, though maybe not in a dramatic way. I mean, I’m still functioning. I don’t want to give the impression that I’ve stopped doing things altogether. I still keep up with the garden, and I still go to book club, and from the outside I probably look more or less like myself.\n\nBut it has started to interfere around the edges, and I notice that. With book club, for example, I still go, but I’m not always as prepared as I used to be. I’ll arrive having read less carefully, or I’ll feel a bit scattered, like I’ve spent all this mental energy worrying about other things and then haven’t had the same space for something I actually enjoy.\n\nAnd with the exhibition planning, that’s probably where I’ve noticed it most. I used to be fairly willing to take on pieces of it, coordinate things, keep track of details. Lately I’ve found myself turning down parts of the planning because it just feels harder to manage than it used to. Not because I don’t care — I do — but because taking on one more thing feels like it will set off this whole chain of worrying.\n\nSo yes, it’s caused distress, and it has changed how much I feel able to take on. I’m still living my life, but not quite as freely as I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any ongoing physical symptoms, like trouble sleeping, headaches, or stomach issues, that seem to be related to the anxiety you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have noticed that, especially in my shoulders and neck. It’s not so much that I think of myself as having a “symptom” at the time, if that makes sense, but by the evening I’ll suddenly realize I’ve been holding myself so tightly all day.\n\nMy shoulders will be up near my ears, and my neck feels stiff, like I’ve been bracing for something. And then I’ll catch myself clenching my jaw, too — sometimes when I’m just trying to read, or when I’m sitting with the garden center activity notes and thinking through who’s bringing what or whether I’ve forgotten some detail. I won’t even notice I’m doing it until my face feels tired or my shoulders ache.\n\nIt’s as if my body is acting like there’s an emergency, even when I’m only sitting in a chair with a book or a list in front of me. It tends to build as the day goes on, and by evening I can feel how tense I’ve been.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety is present even when there are no specific worries or tasks on your mind, or does it only come up when you're thinking about particular responsibilities or events?\n\nPatient: It’s not only when there’s one clear thing in front of me, no. I wish it were that tidy, because then I could say, “All right, I’ll deal with the exhibition schedule,” or “I’ll make one phone call,” and be done with it.\n\nWhat tends to happen is my mind sort of roams. It might start with something quite concrete, like the exhibition schedule — who’s meant to bring what, whether the timing will work, whether I’ve forgotten to confirm something — and then before I know it I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans, and whether she’ll be all right, and then somehow that connects to the house gutters and whether they need fixing before the next heavy rain.\n\nAnd none of those things, taken one at a time, is necessarily enormous. That’s the part that makes me feel a bit foolish saying it out loud. But in my head, small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger if I don’t keep watch over them. So even if I’m not sitting down to do a particular task, there can still be this background scanning going on, looking for what might go wrong next.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel anxious or on edge even when there isn’t anything specific going on or any particular responsibility weighing on you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not constantly, exactly, but more often than I’d like. There are times when I’m just sitting in my own living room — nothing urgent happening, no one waiting on me, no particular decision to make — and I still feel sort of wound up inside.\n\nIt’s almost embarrassing because I’ll think, “What am I even reacting to right now?” I might be on the phone and notice I’m tapping my fingers the whole time, or I’ll get up and straighten a stack of papers, or move a cushion, or fuss with something on the table, not because it truly needs doing but because sitting quietly feels oddly uncomfortable. Like my body wants me to be doing something, even if there isn’t anything that actually needs doing.\n\nSo yes, it can be there even without a clear reason. It’s this jumpy, restless feeling, as if I’m waiting for something to need my attention.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

  "candidates": ["F41.1", "F43.2"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": false
}

It tends to build as the day goes on, and by evening I can feel how tense I’ve been.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety is present even when there are no specific worries or tasks on your mind, or does it only come up when you're thinking about particular responsibilities or events?\n\nPatient: It’s not only when there’s one clear thing in front of me, no. I wish it were that tidy, because then I could say, “All right, I’ll deal with the exhibition schedule,” or “I’ll make one phone call,” and be done with it.\n\nWhat tends to happen is my mind sort of roams. It might start with something quite concrete, like the exhibition schedule — who’s meant to bring what, whether the timing will work, whether I’ve forgotten to confirm something — and then before I know it I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans, and whether she’ll be all right, and then somehow that connects to the house gutters and whether they need fixing before the next heavy rain.\n\nAnd none of those things, taken one at a time, is necessarily enormous. That’s the part that makes me feel a bit foolish saying it out loud. But in my head, small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger if I don’t keep watch over them. So even if I’m not sitting down to do a particular task, there can still be this background scanning going on, looking for what might go wrong next.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel anxious or on edge even when there isn’t anything specific going on or any particular responsibility weighing on you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not constantly, exactly, but more often than I’d like. There are times when I’m just sitting in my own living room — nothing urgent happening, no one waiting on me, no particular decision to make — and I still feel sort of wound up inside.\n\nIt’s almost embarrassing because I’ll think, “What am I even reacting to right now?” I might be on the phone and notice I’m tapping my fingers the whole time, or I’ll get up and straighten a stack of papers, or move a cushion, or fuss with something on the table, not because it truly needs doing but because sitting quietly feels oddly uncomfortable. Like my body wants me to be doing something, even if there isn’t anything that actually needs doing.\n\nSo yes, it can be there even without a clear reason. It’s this jumpy, restless feeling, as if I’m waiting for something to need my attention.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say your anxiety is limited to specific situations or responsibilities, or does it often seem to come up without a clear reason?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s broader than just one situation, I think. It can start with something specific, yes — like the exhibition schedule, making sure I haven’t missed who’s bringing what or whether the timing is going to work — but then it doesn’t stay neatly in that box.

My mind sort of wanders from one concern to the next. I’ll be thinking about that, and then suddenly I’m on to my daughter’s travel plans, wondering if everything will go smoothly for her, and then somehow I’m thinking about the house gutters and whether they need fixing before there’s heavy rain. It sounds almost ridiculous when I list them out, because none of those things is a disaster by itself.

But in the moment, they start to feel connected somehow, like if I don’t keep track of all of them, something small could unravel into something much bigger. So no, I wouldn’t say it’s limited to one responsibility or event. It often feels like my mind is just looking for the next thing to worry over, even when there isn’t one clear reason.

It’s more the same worries coming back and refusing to quiet down.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself mentally going over things in a specific way—like mentally reviewing events in detail, silently repeating certain words or phrases, or trying to 'neutralize' worrisome thoughts with other thoughts—in order to feel less anxious or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think about that carefully, because I don’t want to say no too quickly if I’m missing what you mean.\n\nI do go over things in my mind, definitely. I’ll replay a conversation or a decision or something I’m supposed to remember, and I can get caught in that for longer than is probably useful. But it doesn’t feel like I’m doing it in a specific, set way, if that makes sense. I’m not silently repeating a phrase to cancel something out, or thinking a “good” thought to undo a “bad” one, or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I’m trying to reassure myself by thinking it through one more time.     "content": "Candidate Diseases: \n[\"F41.1\", \"F43.2\"]\n\nInterview Transcript: \nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what's been going on that brought you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally got me to come in is that I can’t seem to turn the worrying off once it gets started. I’ll tell myself, “All right, I’ve made the list, I checked the dates, I’ve done what I can reasonably do,” and part of me knows that should be enough. But then my mind just keeps going back over it anyway, like maybe I missed something, or maybe I should check again, or think it through one more time.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t try to be practical about things—I do. I write things down, I try to be organized, I remind myself there’s nothing more to do at the moment. But the worry sort of keeps running in the background, and sometimes in the foreground, even after I’ve done all the sensible steps. That’s really why I’m here. I’m tired of feeling like I can’t just put it down.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what kinds of things you worry about when your mind keeps going back to them?\n\nPatient: It can be a lot of different things, and that’s part of what makes it feel so silly and exhausting at the same time. My mind doesn’t just stay on one topic. It sort of roams.\n\nFor example, I’ll start thinking about the exhibition schedule — I help keep track of dates and who needs to be where, and whether something has been confirmed — and then before I’ve even finished with that thought, I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans. Like, did she leave enough time between connections, is the weather going to interfere, should I remind her about something even though she’s perfectly capable and doesn’t need me hovering.\n\nAnd then somehow it jumps to the house, like whether the gutters need fixing, or whether I should have called someone already, or if waiting will turn into water damage or some bigger problem. It’s not that any one thing is necessarily terrible on its own. It’s more that small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger if I don’t keep track of them carefully enough. And then I’m off to the races, mentally.\n\nDoctor: When your mind starts worrying like this, do you feel driven to do certain things—like checking, counting, or repeating actions—to try to reduce the anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, exactly. I mean, I do sometimes go back and look at a calendar again, or reread an email, or make another note for myself, but it doesn’t feel like… counting or doing something in a very specific ritual way, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll feel uneasy and think, “Maybe I should just make sure,” and then I check because I’m worried I overlooked something. But I don’t have a particular number of times I have to do it, or a set pattern, or anything like that. It’s more ordinary checking that maybe goes on longer than it needs to because my mind won’t quite let it rest.\n\nI suppose sometimes the checking gives me a little relief, but not for very long, because then I can think of some other angle I didn’t consider. So… no, I don’t think I’m driven to repeat actions in that kind of way. It’s mostly the thinking and rethinking that gets me stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel constant or almost daily, and have they been happening for most days over the past several months?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I’d say it’s been most days. Not every single minute of every day, but close enough that it feels like it’s become part of the background of my life.\n\nIt really started about seven months ago, I think—shortly after my friend died. We had been involved in some projects together, and after she was gone I felt this need to keep things moving, to make sure nothing fell apart or got forgotten. At first I thought it was just grief, or responsibility, or both, and maybe some of it was. But then the worrying just sort of stayed. It kept attaching itself to one thing after another.\n\nSo yes, for the past several months—about seven months—it’s been happening on most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any thoughts that come into your mind that you don’t want or that feel intrusive, that cause you anxiety or distress, even if you try to push them away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I usually understand “intrusive thoughts,” at least. I mean, I do have thoughts I don’t want, in the sense that I wish my brain would stop bringing up all these worries again and again. But they don’t feel like strange thoughts that pop in out of nowhere, or images that shock me, or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like I start worrying about something ordinary, and then it gets sticky. I’ll try to move on and tell myself, “You’ve already thought about this,” but then my mind circles back anyway. So the thoughts are unwanted, yes, because they’re tiring and upsetting, but they feel connected to real-life concerns rather than like some separate, intrusive thing.\n\nI’m not sure if that distinction makes sense. I may be splitting hairs. But no, I haven’t really noticed thoughts that feel alien or bizarre or like I’m fighting off something completely out of character. It’s more the same worries coming back and refusing to quiet down.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself mentally going over things in a specific way—like mentally reviewing events in detail, silently repeating certain words or phrases, or trying to 'neutralize' worrisome thoughts with other thoughts—in order to feel less anxious or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think about that carefully, because I don’t want to say no too quickly if I’m missing what you mean.\n\nI do go over things in my mind, definitely. I’ll replay a conversation or a decision or something I’m supposed to remember, and I can get caught in that for longer than is probably useful. But it doesn’t feel like I’m doing it in a specific, set way, if that makes sense. I’m not silently repeating a phrase to cancel something out, or thinking a “good” thought to undo a “bad” one, or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I’m trying to reassure myself by thinking it through one more time. And then one more time. But there isn’t a rule to it. I don’t have to review it in a certain order, or until it feels exactly right, or to prevent something terrible in a superstitious way. It’s just that my mind keeps wanting another pass at it.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I mentally review things, yes, but not in the kind of ritualized or neutralizing way you’re describing. It feels more like worry that won’t settle down.\n\nDoctor: Has this constant worrying affected your ability to function in your daily life, like at work, with your family, or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, though I’d say it’s not… it hasn’t stopped my life, exactly. I’m still doing things. I still keep up with the garden, and I still go to book club, so from the outside I probably look mostly the same.\n\nBut I can tell I’m not functioning quite like I used to. I’ll arrive at book club less prepared than I normally would, like I’ve read the chapter but haven’t really gathered my thoughts, or I’ve forgotten the notes I meant to bring. And with the exhibition planning, I’ve started stepping back from pieces of it. I’ll turn down certain tasks or say I can’t take on a part of it, not because I don’t care, but because it just feels harder to manage than it used to.\n\nIt’s subtle, maybe, but it bothers me because I used to be someone who could keep those things organized. Now I still show up, but I feel less capable and less ready.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like restlessness, feeling on edge, fatigue, or muscle tension that seem to go along with this worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have noticed that, mostly as this sort of jumpy, wound-up feeling. It’s not dramatic, exactly, but it’s there. I’ll be sitting in my own living room, where there’s no reason to feel keyed up, and I’ll realize I’m tapping my fingers on the arm of the chair or on the phone while I’m talking to someone.\n\nAnd sometimes I just can’t seem to sit still comfortably. I’ll get up and straighten a cushion or move a stack of papers or adjust something on a shelf, not because it really needs doing, but because sitting quietly makes me feel… uneasy, I suppose. Like my body is looking for something to do while my mind is still worrying.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m pacing around the house all day, but there is this sense of being on edge in small ways. It’s almost embarrassing, because it’s my own home and nothing is happening, but I still feel wound up.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, would you say that your level of worry and anxiety has gone beyond what would be expected in response to the situations you're concerned about, or has it felt more like a natural reaction to real-life stresses?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, looking back, it has gone beyond what the situations really call for. I mean, the things I worry about are real things, so I can always make an argument to myself that I’m just being responsible. There is an exhibition schedule, and my daughter does have travel plans, and the gutters may actually need looking at. Those aren’t imaginary.\n\nBut the way my mind treats them feels out of proportion. It doesn’t just stay with one concern and solve it. It sort of roams from one thing to the next—the exhibition schedule, then whether my daughter’s flight will go smoothly, then whether the house gutters are going to turn into some expensive problem—and before I know it, very ordinary things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger.\n\nThat’s the part that makes me think it isn’t just a natural reaction. It’s like small uncertainties don’t stay small in my head. They gather weight. And even when I know, logically, that none of these things is a disaster at the moment, my worry behaves as if they might become one.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself especially worried about your health or physical symptoms, to the point where it causes you a lot of anxiety even if medical evaluations have told you there's nothing seriously wrong?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not in that specific way. I mean, at my age I suppose you do notice your body more than you used to, and if something aches or feels odd, I might think, “Oh, what’s that about?” But I wouldn’t say I get especially consumed by health worries or keep thinking something serious is being missed.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll make a mental note of something and then it usually passes into the background. I don’t think I’ve had that pattern of being reassured and then still being terribly anxious about it. At least, not that I can think of. My worries tend to attach themselves more to responsibilities and things that feel like they could go wrong, rather than to my health specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it significantly difficult to control the worry, even when you know it might not be reasonable or helpful?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s really the thing that brings me in, I suppose. It isn’t just that I worry — everyone worries — it’s that once it starts, I can’t seem to turn it off.\n\nI’ll tell myself, quite firmly sometimes, “All right, you’ve made the list, you’ve checked the dates, you’ve done what can reasonably be done.” And for a few minutes I might believe that. But then the worry sort of starts up again anyway, almost like a radio left on in another room. I know it isn’t necessarily helping anything, and I can even see that I’m going over the same ground, but I still can’t quite let it go.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I don’t feel as though I’m choosing to keep thinking about it. I’ll try to redirect myself or say, “Enough now,” but my mind keeps circling back, looking for one more thing I might have missed.\n\nDoctor: Would you say the anxiety and worry you're experiencing has caused you significant distress or has it interfered with your ability to live your life the way you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has, though maybe not in a dramatic way. I mean, I’m still functioning. I don’t want to give the impression that I’ve stopped doing things altogether. I still keep up with the garden, and I still go to book club, and from the outside I probably look more or less like myself.\n\nBut it has started to interfere around the edges, and I notice that. With book club, for example, I still go, but I’m not always as prepared as I used to be. I’ll arrive having read less carefully, or I’ll feel a bit scattered, like I’ve spent all this mental energy worrying about other things and then haven’t had the same space for something I actually enjoy.\n\nAnd with the exhibition planning, that’s probably where I’ve noticed it most. I used to be fairly willing to take on pieces of it, coordinate things, keep track of details. Lately I’ve found myself turning down parts of the planning because it just feels harder to manage than it used to. Not because I don’t care — I do — but because taking on one more thing feels like it will set off this whole chain of worrying.\n\nSo yes, it’s caused distress, and it has changed how much I feel able to take on. I’m still living my life, but not quite as freely as I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any ongoing physical symptoms, like trouble sleeping, headaches, or stomach issues, that seem to be related to the anxiety you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have noticed that, especially in my shoulders and neck. It’s not so much that I think of myself as having a “symptom” at the time, if that makes sense, but by the evening I’ll suddenly realize I’ve been holding myself so tightly all day.\n\nMy shoulders will be up near my ears, and my neck feels stiff, like I’ve been bracing for something. And then I’ll catch myself clenching my jaw, too — sometimes when I’m just trying to read, or when I’m sitting with the garden center activity notes and thinking through who’s bringing what or whether I’ve forgotten some detail. I won’t even notice I’m doing it until my face feels tired or my shoulders ache.\n\nIt’s as if my body is acting like there’s an emergency, even when I’m only sitting in a chair with a book or a list in front of me. It tends to build as the day goes on, and by evening I can feel how tense I’ve been.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety is present even when there are no specific worries or tasks on your mind, or does it only come up when you're thinking about particular responsibilities or events?\n\nPatient: It’s not only when there’s one clear thing in front of me, no. I wish it were that tidy, because then I could say, “All right, I’ll deal with the exhibition schedule,” or “I’ll make one phone call,” and be done with it.\n\nWhat tends to happen is my mind sort of roams. It might start with something quite concrete, like the exhibition schedule — who’s meant to bring what, whether the timing will work, whether I’ve forgotten to confirm something — and then before I know it I’m thinking about my daughter’s travel plans, and whether she’ll be all right, and then somehow that connects to the house gutters and whether they need fixing before the next heavy rain.\n\nAnd none of those things, taken one at a time, is necessarily enormous. That’s the part that makes me feel a bit foolish saying it out loud. But in my head, small things start to feel as if they could unravel into something much bigger if I don’t keep watch over them. So even if I’m not sitting down to do a particular task, there can still be this background scanning going on, looking for what might go wrong next.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel anxious or on edge even when there isn’t anything specific going on or any particular responsibility weighing on you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not constantly, exactly, but more often than I’d like. There are times when I’m just sitting in my own living room — nothing urgent happening, no one waiting on me, no particular decision to make — and I still feel sort of wound up inside.\n\nIt’s almost embarrassing because I’ll think, “What am I even reacting to right now?” I might be on the phone and notice I’m tapping my fingers the whole time, or I’ll get up and straighten a stack of papers, or move a cushion, or fuss with something on the table, not because it truly needs doing but because sitting quietly feels oddly uncomfortable. Like my body wants me to be doing something, even if there isn’t anything that actually needs doing.\n\nSo yes, it can be there even without a clear reason. It’s this jumpy, restless feeling, as if I’m waiting for something to need my attention.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your anxiety is limited to specific situations or responsibilities, or does it often seem to come up without a clear reason?\n\nPatient: It’s broader than just one situation, I think. It can start with something specific, yes — like the exhibition schedule, making sure I haven’t missed who’s bringing what or whether the timing is going to work — but then it doesn’t stay neatly in that box.\n\nMy mind sort of wanders from one concern to the next. I’ll be thinking about that, and then suddenly I’m on to my daughter’s travel plans, wondering if everything will go smoothly for her, and then somehow I’m thinking about the house gutters and whether they need fixing before there’s heavy rain. It sounds almost ridiculous when I list them out, because none of those things is a disaster by itself.\n\nBut in the moment, they start to feel connected somehow, like if I don’t keep track of all of them, something small could unravel into something much bigger. So no, I wouldn’t say it’s limited to one responsibility or event. It often feels like my mind is just looking for the next thing to worry over, even when there isn’t one clear reason."
